Heads only (row T11.16). **Test views:** rendered at zero offset, a held-out camera's score sets the canonical geometry against that camera's own version of the person (reading 3): a better surface can score lower | 60,000 iterations; about an hour on an A100 with the generation (row T11.16). Here: a small network evaluated on the visible primitives each iteration; not measured |
| **AniGS' views as frames** (Qiu et al., 2024, arXiv 2412.02684): inconsistent generated views of a person treated as the frames of a sequence, with a deformation field per view over a canonical model | SAM 2 for the person's masks: **pass** (section 3.3, SAM 2's row). It also takes normal maps from its own generator, and its initial mesh from SMPL-X, a body template, which this survey records and does not screen (decision 1) | No code repository is named in the paper: paper only (row T11.17) | A change to a quantity or to motion, as T2Bs' row. AniGS' subject holds one pose, and the view index takes the place of time; here time is real, so the view would be a second axis of deformation beside t, which is T2Bs' form | Nothing new for the deformation. Its normal loss needs rendered normals (section 2.4, cells 4a and 4d) and belongs to section 3.4 | A HexPlane field, with a total-variation loss for smoothness across views, gives each Gaussian a change of position, rotation and scale per view: a field, so clones and relocated copies need no new state (E11, E17). The rotation and scale offsets go against §1.1's constants and are not what a disagreement in position asks for | It names the setting for a person: multi-view images of one person from a video model vary subtly between views, and plain 3DGS on them degrades. It takes the first frame as canonical; as T2Bs' row for the canonical camera private scene A lacks. It supervises with person masks and with normal maps generated beside the images. It could absorb the person's part, by giving every view its own geometry and reading the result at one view (row T11.17) | A person mask per image (SAM 2; supplied masks would do). The normal maps and the mesh are its generator's and its body template's | Nothing isolates the deformation: the evidence is a figure of the inconsistency and comparisons with other avatar methods, and the ablations isolate the normal loss, the anisotropy regularizer and the mesh initialization (row T11.17). **Test views:** as T2Bs' row | About 5 minutes of optimization on an RTX 3090, for one static avatar (row T11.17) |
| **4Real's per-frame deformation** (Yu et al., NeurIPS 2024, arXiv 2406.07472): the inconsistency between views as a small deformation per view of a canonical model, with the cameras fine-tuned | none: no estimator for the deformation and its small-motion loss. Its later stage distils from the video model that made the views, the generator, which would have to run inside training and which this survey does not screen | No code repository is named in the paper: paper only (row T11.18) | A change to a quantity or to motion, as T2Bs' row, with offsets of position and orientation only; and cameras fine-tuned jointly, as ViDAR's first row | As T2Bs' row for the deformation; the camera's gradient as ViDAR's first row (row T11.20) | As T2Bs' row. 4Real states the ambiguity: many pairs of deformation and Gaussians render the same images, so an L1 loss at weight 0.01 keeps the offsets small, and without it the model overfits the inconsistent video and is noisy (row T11.18). An L1 pull towards zero needs no canonical camera if every camera is deformed alike: the model is then the geometry that needs the least total offset (this ticket's reading, no source) | It names two kinds of inconsistency in a generated multi-view video: consistent in time but geometrically wrong, and residual motion of objects. It assumes one canonical frame, taken from the reference video. Figure 9: without the deformation, the regions that are not geometrically consistent are underfitted; without the small-motion loss, overfitting and noise. It could absorb the person's part by the deformation and the background's by the camera fine-tuning (row T11.18) | None | Qualitative ablations (its Figures 8 and 9) and a user study; no metric against ground truth (row T11.18). **Test views:** as T2Bs' row | One A100; its distillation step takes about 200 ms with its own low-resolution model (row T11.18). Here as T2Bs' row; the distillation is not available without the generator |
| **RIGI's two-model uncertainty** (Wang et al., 2024, arXiv 2411.18866): two models trained at once on the same inconsistent images; the difference of their renders weights the loss per pixel | none: no estimator for the uncertainty. Its loss also has LPIPS at 0.5: **fail** (this section's estimators table); not needed, as CAT4D's row | No code repository is named in the paper: paper only (row T11.19) | Nearest class: a loss on today's primitives, with reading 2's change: a continuous weight per pixel, the L1 term divided by exp(λU) with λU added. The class leaves out the second model: two sets of primitives, two optimizers and two growth and relocation states trained side by side, of which one is the result (reading 1). With the field off, untouched | Two renders per iteration, one per model, of the same camera; nothing new from either backend | The primitives are untouched. The signal is the randomness between two trainings, and RIGI initializes each model at random. Here both models would start from the same init points (§1.3 Initialization) and differ only by the seed's draws in growth, split and relocation (E11, E17). Section 2.6 found two seeds to differ by 0.001 to 0.002 in the far-5 cm share, and the notes by ±0.01 dB at the centre test view (section 2.1). Whether two seeds differ per pixel where the shell is, and agree elsewhere, is not measured; the two default checkpoints of section 2.6, `A_default_s0` and `A_default_s1`, could answer it without training (this ticket's reading) | It needs no trusted camera and no pretrained network, and does not decide beforehand which content is wrong: the only down-weighting row of this family of which all three hold. It names the mechanism H2 assumes: conflicting pseudo-labels make a Gaussian model densify redundant points to satisfy some viewpoints. Its stated trade is H2's too: a larger λ removes more artifacts and leaves the inconsistent regions smoother and blurrier. Its inconsistencies are local, along the boundaries of a static object generated from one image; a subject that differs everywhere between cameras is not its setting (row T11.19). It could lower the weight of the person's part; whether it would then blur or remove the person is reading 2's question | None | Objects generated from one image with SV3D; qualitative ablations of λ and of the uncertainty's design, where a learnable variance per Gaussian was unstable and an ensemble gave similar results at a higher cost. No table isolates the weighting (row T11.19) | Not stated (row T11.19). Here: about twice the training cost and memory, two models of up to N_max primitives each (D-44) |
| **RobustNeRF's trimmed loss** (Sabour et al., 2023, arXiv 2302.00833): pixels whose residual is above the median are left out, decided by patch | none: no estimator | No code repository of the method is linked from the project page: paper only (row T11.23) | A loss on today's primitives, with reading 2's change to `total_loss`. The mask is computed in training from the residual alone, so §2.2 need not change. With the field off, E1 to E17 and the §3 tests are untouched | Nothing new: the rendered image and its residual | The primitives are untouched (reading 2). The estimator sorts the residuals of a batch of rays drawn from all images; with one image per iteration (D-26) the quantile has to be tracked across iterations, as SpotLessSplats' row does. At the median it discards half of the pixels whatever they are, which the paper names as its inefficiency: slightly worse and slower on clean data (row T11.23) | It assumes distractors that do not persist through the capture, in large connected regions, and inliers in the majority. It needs no trusted camera and no network. On private scene A the largest residuals are the person's, in every image: the trimmed half would be the person's region first (reading 2). The paper notes a smaller gain on its scenes with errors in the camera parameters and imperceptible appearance changes; a trimmed loss also trims what a pose error misplaces, so the background's part would feed it too. It sets milder robust kernels (Charbonnier, Geman-McClure) aside: they do not separate outliers from detail not yet learned (row T11.23) | None | Its own scenes with paired clean and cluttered captures, and synthetic ones: 1.3 to 4.7 dB over mip-NeRF 360 variants; as the share of cluttered images grows, mip-NeRF 360 falls from 33 to 25 dB and RobustNeRF stays above 31 dB. An ablation on one scene isolates the trimmed estimator, the smoothing and the patch window (row T11.23) | On a par with mip-NeRF 360 once the patch window is used; slower on clean data (row T11.23). Here: a quantile per iteration |
| **Known masks left out of the loss** (the oracle form of RobustNeRF's loss, Sabour et al., 2023; SPIn-NeRF's "Masked NeRF" baseline, Mirzaei et al., CVPR 2023): the pixels a given mask marks contribute nothing | none: no estimator. The masks are given; for private scene A's placeholder pixels a rule on colour computed one per frame (section 2.5) | none: the technique is a formula in the papers read (rows T11.23, T11.27) | A loss on today's primitives, with reading 2's change in full: `total_loss` takes a mask, and §2.2's manifest allows `mask` on a training image, which it refuses today. How the mask enters SSIM and the perceptual term has to be fixed, and the papers read give three forms: multiply the per-pixel L1 and DSSIM maps by the mask (WildGaussians, row T11.13); zero the masked pixels in the render and in the image before the standard loss (PDF-GS, row T11.28), as D-67's black fill does for the dynamic-region metrics; draw the loss's samples from unmasked pixels only (SPIn-NeRF, row T11.27). With the field off, untouched | Nothing new: the rendered image | The primitives are untouched. With 0.263% of one camera's pixels masked and eight other cameras on the same surface, E10's g and E8 hardly change: the opposite case from a mask that covers the person (reading 2) | It assumes the wrong pixels are known beforehand and that other images see what they hide. That is the placeholder pixels' case: one camera, all inside the person's crop, a mask per frame computed, and the dense matcher already admits nothing there (section 2.5). No trusted camera is needed. It absorbs the placeholder pixels and nothing else. SPIn-NeRF's baseline shows the limit: where no image supervises the masked region, a NeRF trained on the unmasked pixels alone is the worst of its table (LPIPS 0.6030 against 0.4654, row T11.27) | A mask per training image, as a file. Computed already for private scene A, by a rule on each pixel's colour, in seconds (section 2.5) | Nothing isolates masking a fraction of a percent of one camera. RobustNeRF writes the oracle loss as the reference its estimator approximates (row T11.23); SPIn-NeRF's Table 2 holds the unmasked-pixels-only baseline (row T11.27). **Test views:** with `cam0` as the test view, E16 scores its placeholder pixels too. The PRD's starting proposal leaves them out as the kept-pixel PSNR leaves out flagged pixels (Q-233); a test image may carry a `mask` today, but D-67's mask selects a region to score, not pixels to drop | None beyond a multiplication per pixel |
| **Bilateral grids per view** (Wang et al., 2024, arXiv 2406.00448): a locally affine colour correction per training view, between the render and the loss, dropped after training | none: no estimator | Apache-2.0 license file at commit ebf1a96 (row T11.24): readable. Only the license file was opened | Nearest class: a loss on today's primitives, since the correction sits between `render` and E7 and the primitives are unchanged. The class leaves out a trainable grid per training view, its optimizer and its total-variation term (reading 1). The exported model is a plain one. With the field off, untouched | Nothing new: it is applied to the rendered image | The primitives are untouched. A grid per training view is 1,080 grids on private scene A; per camera, nine. E7's render is not clamped at 1, and the grid is looked up by a luminance in [0, 1], so the lookup needs a rule above 1 (this ticket's reading) | It assumes images that agree in geometry and differ by a smooth colour processing per view, which it names as a cause of floaters. It absorbs colour, the part nobody has measured on private scene A. It trusts no camera: the field bakes an average processing and each grid holds its view's departure from it. Its limits are stated: it does not model transient objects and fails on in-the-wild collections; and without a total-variation loss at a high weight the grids take up low-frequency scene content and leave ghosts, a correction explaining away what it should not (row T11.24) | None | Seven night scenes with exposure varied per view: 27.53 dB against Zip-NeRF's 25.17, and 26.54 with a latent code per image. **Test views:** each output is aligned to its ground-truth image by a per-channel affine transform before the metrics, because the test images carry their own processing (row T11.24); under E16 and D-67 that is a fit to the test image (reading 3) | The backbone's training time, by the paper's account (row T11.24). Here: a grid lookup per pixel per iteration; not measured |
| **3R-GS' pose optimization** (Huang et al., 2025, arXiv 2504.04294): camera corrections from one shared MLP, held by an epipolar loss on precomputed matches | MASt3R-SfM for the initial cameras and the matches: **fail**, non-commercial and share-alike (section 3.3, MASt3R's row). Not needed: the package's dense matcher gives matches with a certainty (D-58, Q-106; its weights' terms are rows 282 and 283), and section 2.5 measured the epipolar residual from them | No license file answered in the repository at the common names (row T11.25): documentation only, and the method is the paper's | Nearest class: a loss on today's primitives, as ViDAR's first row; the class leaves out the camera embeddings, the MLP and their optimizer (reading 1). Its epipolar term is a loss on cameras and matches, not on the render: a term E7 and E9 do not have. Its third practice, 3DGS as MCMC sampling, is where E11 comes from (D-15), and its regularizers on opacity and scale are section 3.2's. With the field off, untouched | The camera's gradient for the rendering loss, as ViDAR's first row (row T11.20). The epipolar term needs no render | The primitives are untouched. The epipolar term is section 2.5's measure turned into a loss: the epipolar distance of confident matches, over all pairs, every iteration. On private scene A it would have to read the background's matches only: the person's matches miss the epipolar geometry for another reason, and in three pairs the turn that suits the background suits the person less (section 2.5) | It assumes a static scene whose images agree, seen by cameras that are slightly off. It could absorb the background's part. Two cautions from the papers read. Its best configuration ends at a rotation error of 0.195° on Tanks and Temples, from 0.805° (row T11.25), which is the size of the turns section 2.5 fitted: joint optimization is not shown to resolve errors that small. And AligNeRF finds that optimizing poses jointly blurs the whole scene when a part of it differs subtly between views (row T11.26): the pose update should not see the person | Matches between camera pairs, precomputed; `roma_init` computes the dense matcher's already (D-22) | Tanks and Temples, Mip-NeRF 360, DTU. Table 6 isolates each practice in rotation error, trajectory error and PSNR: 3DGS with pose optimization 0.805°, 0.124 m, 20.49 dB; with MCMC and the MLP refiner 0.353°, 0.025 m, 23.61 dB; with the epipolar loss too 0.195°, 0.015 m, 24.02 dB (row T11.25). **Test views:** the test cameras' poses are optimized on the test images before scoring (row T11.25; reading 3) | Called minimal overhead, on an RTX 4090 with one view per iteration (row T11.25). Here: a few hundred matches per pair per iteration for the epipolar term, with no render |
| **AligNeRF's alignment-aware loss** (Jiang et al., 2022, arXiv 2211.09682): a rendered patch is compared with the best-matching shifted patch of the training image | none for the patch matching. Beside it the paper has a feature-space term on the first block of VGG-19, weights not stated in the parts read (row T11.26); D-89's `vgg16_oxford` is the VGG the package admits | No code repository is named in the paper: paper only (row T11.26) | A loss on today's primitives: another form of E7's pixel term, in which each rendered patch may shift by up to 8 px against the training image, at a penalty per pixel of shift. It reads `RenderOutput.image` and the training image only, so `total_loss`'s signature can stay; with the field on, E7's form [P] changes. With it off, untouched | Nothing new: the rendered image | The primitives are untouched. It is a 2D shift of the target per camera, time and patch, chosen anew at each iteration, with no parameters: what the shell does with primitives (section 2.6), the loss does by moving the target. Section 2.5's residuals after the turn, 0.9 to 4.0 px at the median and 2.6 to 12.4 px at the 90th percentile, lie within or near its ± 8 px window; they bound the disagreement from below only. The minimum over shifts passes a gradient through the chosen patch alone, and E10's g then follows the shifted target | It names two sources of misalignment, cameras that are slightly off and objects that move slightly between views, and treats both alike, without trusting any view. It could absorb the background's part and the person's, as far as they are shifts of a few pixels, rigid over a patch. It does not say where the surface is: a model that satisfies every camera up to a shift has no reason to prefer one geometry, and only the paper's penalty, 0.01 per pixel of shift, pulls towards the unshifted target (this ticket's reading) | None | Mip-NeRF 360's outdoor scenes at 2560 × 1680. Table 4 isolates it on one scene: + 0.10 dB on the standard test set and + 0.25 dB on a test set warped onto the model's geometry (row T11.26). Small gains, on a NeRF, on real captures. **Test views:** the paper scores on warped test images beside the original ones, because a misaligned test image biases the comparison: the nearest thing in this family to a way of scoring when no view is aligned (reading 3) | The same number of rays as standard training, by its account (row T11.26). Here: full images are rendered, so the search is a correlation of render and image over a window; not measured |
| **AligNeRF's iterative re-alignment** (the same paper, its analysis): the training images warped onto the model's geometry by optical flow, and the model retrained | PWC-Net for the flow: **fail**, non-commercial (this section's estimators table). Section 3.3's passing flow and tracking estimators are TAPIR's family and AllTracker | As the row above (row T11.26) | None of the five classes: a preprocessing of the training images between two trainings, which changes no primitive, loss or render (reading 1). In the package's terms it is a second scene manifest with warped images and a second `ftgs train`; no spec item changes. It alters the data, which is the owner's call | Nothing new: it reads finished renders of the training cameras | The primitives are untouched. It needs a first model whose renders are sharp where the images are misaligned, and the paper notes that blur hurts the flow and takes two or three rounds. Here the first model would be the one with the shell, which reproduces each training camera (28.1 dB on the centre camera's crop when it trains, 24.1 dB when held out, section 2.1): the flow from its render to the training image would be near zero and correct nothing. The first model would have to be one that cannot fit each camera separately (this ticket's reading) | It absorbs misalignment whatever its cause, by moving each image's pixels to where a consensus model renders them: no view is trusted above the model. It assumes the model's geometry is roughly right and the texture displaced | A flow field per training image and round, from a flow estimator on pairs of render and image; supplied flow would do | One scene: + 0.62 dB for the large model and + 0.23 dB for the standard one, on the warped test set (its Table 1). The paper calls it very time-consuming and proposes the row above in its place (row T11.26) | Each round is a full training, a render and a flow of every training image: two or three trainings (row T11.26) |
| **SPIn-NeRF's split loss** (Mirzaei et al., CVPR 2023, arXiv 2211.12254): a mask per image, a pixel loss outside it and a perceptual loss alone inside it | LPIPS inside the mask: **fail** (this section's estimators table); not needed: D-89's networks. Its masks come from its own multi-view segmentation, and a 2D inpainter made the inconsistent content; neither is needed where the region is given | CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (row T11.27): non-commercial, so documentation only; the method is the paper's | A loss on today's primitives, with reading 2's change: a region per training image inside which L1 and SSIM are off and the perceptual term alone acts. The region could be the person's crop, a fixed rectangle per camera in the owner's tools (section 2.5), which needs no mask file and so no change to §2.2. It differs from the notes' trial of `perc_net` (section 2.1) in what it takes away: the notes changed the network and kept L1 and SSIM on the person. With the field off, untouched | Nothing new: the rendered image | The primitives are untouched. Inside the region E10's g comes from the perceptual term alone (reading 2). SPIn-NeRF renders patches inside the mask's bounding box for LPIPS; here the perceptual network already reads the whole render (D-89) | It names the setting in small: views inpainted one by one are inconsistent in 3D, and a pixel loss on them gives blur. It trusts the unmasked pixels fully, and the masked region only up to its texture. It needs to know where the views disagree; on private scene A that is the person's crop, known with no trusted camera. Its stated failure is the case to fear: the perceptual loss handles inconsistencies of texture and may not converge where the views disagree semantically (row T11.27); section 2.5's disagreement is one of position, of millimetres to centimetres, which lies between the two | A region per training image: the person's crop, or a person mask (SAM 2 passes, section 3.3) | Its own dataset of scenes with and without an object, LPIPS and FID (Table 2): inpainted views under a pixel loss 0.5699 and 238.33, the split loss 0.4654 and 156.64. The two methods differ in more than the loss, so the comparison isolates the perceptual term only loosely (row T11.27) | Not stated for the loss alone. Here: no added cost, since the perceptual term already runs |
| **PDF-GS' progressive filtering** (2026, arXiv 2604.12580): train, mask where render and image differ in feature space, retrain from the initialization; three times, then a last pass | DINOv3 features for the discrepancy: **fail** as stated (this section's estimators table). DINOv2's features, which pass (row 367), are what D-89 already loads | Gaussian-Splatting License (row T11.28): research and evaluation only, so documentation only; the method is the paper's | A loss on today's primitives, with reading 2's change, inside an outer loop the class leaves out: several trainings in sequence from the same init points, with masks made between them from the previous model's renders (reading 1). Its first phase trains on SSIM alone, which existing fields give (`loss.lambda_img` 0, `loss.lambda_perc` 0), and updates colours only every N iterations, a schedule §1.3's optimizer lacks. With the field off, untouched | Nothing new: finished renders of the training cameras | The primitives are untouched; each phase restarts from the init points, as every `ftgs train` does | Its signal is the model's own failure to reproduce what is not consistent across views: such content fades in the renders, and the feature discrepancy with the training image finds it. That needs a model that cannot represent the inconsistent content. A static 3DGS cannot; today's primitives can, and the shell is how: each training camera is reproduced (28.1 dB on the centre camera's crop when it trains, 24.1 dB when held out, section 2.1), so the discrepancy on training images would be small where the cameras disagree most (this ticket's reading). Where it did fire, it would fire on the person (reading 2). It assumes transient distractors in a static scene, and trusts no camera | None beforehand; the masks are made between phases | NeRF On-the-go and RobustNeRF's scenes. The number of phases is ablated: the largest gain is from one to two, and it saturates after three (row T11.28) | 40,000 iterations in all and 24 to 28 minutes a scene, about a single-pass robust method's (row T11.28). Here: as many trainings as phases, if each is a full run |
| **EG4D's multiscale training and colour transform** (2024, arXiv 2405.18132): training images downsampled by a random factor, and an affine colour transform per Gaussian and timestamp | none: no estimator | No license file answered in the repository at the common names (row T11.29): documentation only, and the method is the paper's | The multiscale part is a loss on today's primitives: render and training image are both taken at a random lower resolution, which is a camera with scaled intrinsics and a resized image, with no change to `render` or `total_loss`; the training image is then no longer the manifest's (D-26), a logged change. The colour transform is of the class of WildGaussians' second row, with the timestamp in the image's place. With the field off, untouched | Nothing new: `render` takes the camera's size and intrinsics | The primitives are untouched. Whether E10's g, read in NDC units (§1.3 Growth), is indifferent to the render's resolution was not checked | It names the setting: multi-view videos generated from one image carry high-frequency noise at particular views and times, and a 4D Gaussian model fits it, by per-frame deformation and more Gaussians, which the paper calls view- and frame-overfitting. Its answer withholds the detail the model would overfit: lower training PSNR, the same or higher test PSNR. It trusts no view. It absorbs disagreement in fine detail only: a disagreement in position of several pixels (section 2.5) survives a small downsampling. Its colour transform takes one timestamp as the reference at test time, as T2Bs' row takes one view (row T11.29) | None | Objects generated from single images. Qualitative, but for one training curve with and without the multiscale renderer (its Figure 7) (row T11.29) | Not stated (row T11.29). Here: renders at a lower resolution cost less per iteration |

## 4. The finalists and review 1

*Ticket #13's section.* One screening table and one estimators table, consolidated from sections 3.1 to 3.6; H2's premise, answered with the probes' figures (sections 2.5 to 2.7); two to four finalists, at least one of them only a loss on today's primitives, each with the reason it was chosen; the proposed criteria for training tests, starting from the PRD's proposal; per finalist, the route for trying it and its GPU hours; the owner's answers at review 1, with their date.

## 5. The test plan

*Ticket #14's section.* For each finalist the owner kept at review 1, before anything trains: the spec items it would touch, by identifier, and how; the fields of the training configuration it would add, with their defaults, and whether the default leaves E1 to E17 and the §3 tests untouched; the prior maps it needs and how they would reach training; the runs, with the criteria agreed at review 1. A sketched signature or field is a proposal for the owner's spec revision, not a Q-n candidate.

## 6. The results

*Ticket #15's section.* For every finalist review 1 cleared to train, its results against the agreed criteria; or the record that none was cleared, or that a finalist waits for its spec revision and is tested later behind its field.

## 7. The recommendation

*Ticket #15's section.* Which techniques to adopt, each as a field of the training configuration that defaults to off, or that none should be adopted.

## 8. What adopting it would change

*Ticket #15's section.* For each recommended technique, over the PRD's list: §1.1 and E5 to E6 if a quantity or the primitive's shape changes, and E1 to E4 if orientation varies with time; E7 and E9 for new loss terms; §1.3 Growth, E10, E11 and E17 for constrained primitives; §1.3 Initialization and E15 for initialization from prior maps; §2.1's `RenderOutput`, `render` and `LossConfig`; §2.2's `ftgs-scene` and `ftgs-if`; §2.3 for any estimator, weights or dependency; U-7, U-8 and U-12; §3.3's adapter contract; §2.4 for cost.

## 9. The sources

*Ticket #15's section.* The clean-room record in full, completing the opening's: the source-log rows of every ticket of the PRD, what was read only as documentation, what was not opened, and any incident.
